I don’t usually talk to people that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any serious way. I mean, I have my quieter days, but I still try to keep up with things. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you had other instances recently where you’ve felt like you lost control of your temper or reacted more strongly than you intended?\n\nPatient: Mostly just that one time, doctor—the thing with the crooked tablecloth. I raised my voice at that young volunteer more than I meant to, and it bothered me because I really don’t usually speak to people that way. I suppose it felt like I lost hold of myself for a moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other situations lately where you’ve felt overwhelmed by your emotions or acted impulsively, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Well… not really other situations, no. The main thing is still that moment with the young volunteer and the tablecloth being crooked. I got sharper than I should have, and I don’t like that I spoke that way. It was brief, but it felt… unlike me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other moments recently, even small ones, where you felt a sudden rush of anger or irritation that was hard to control, even if you didn’t act on it?\n\nPatient: I keep coming back to that same incident, honestly. I don’t think I’ve had other flashes like that—not that I can think of. What brought me in was that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I really don’t usually talk to people that way. It was embarrassing… I hope I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself reacting more emotionally than usual to small frustrations or stressors lately, even if it didn’t result in an outburst?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, maybe a little, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. The main thing I keep thinking about is when I snapped at that young volunteer over the crooked tablecloth. I raised my voice, and that’s just not how I usually talk to people. It felt like too much for such a small thing, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Since that incident with the tablecloth, have you had any other moments where you felt a sudden urge to lash out or became overwhelmed with frustration, even if you didn’t actually raise your voice or act on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been another moment like that, no… at least not one that stands out. It’s really that tablecloth incident that bothered me. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over something so small, and I don’t usually speak to people that way. I know it wasn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any urges recently to act in a way that you knew was inappropriate or impulsive, even if you managed to stop yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I wouldn’t say urges exactly, no. The thing I keep thinking about is that I did snap at that younger volunteer over the tablecloth being crooked. It was such a small thing, and I raised my voice before I really caught myself. I don’t usually talk to people like that, so… that’s what bothered me enough to come in. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Since the tablecloth incident, have you found yourself feeling more irritable or easily annoyed by small things, even if you didn’t act on those feelings?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s just that one incident that’s been weighing on me. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I really don’t usually talk to people that way. I suppose that means I was more easily annoyed than I’d like to admit, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any other times in the past, even before the tablecloth incident, where you felt you reacted with sudden anger or frustration that seemed out of proportion to the situation, even if it didn’t come up in our conversation so far?\n\nPatient: Not really, doctor… not that I can think of. I mean, what brought me in was that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I just don’t usually talk to people that way. It felt out of proportion after the fact, and I was embarrassed by it. But before that, I can’t say there’s been some pattern of me doing that.\n\nDoctor: Before the tablecloth incident, have you ever had moments where you felt a sudden emotional reaction that you barely managed to control, even if it didn’t result in an outburst or action?\n\nPatient: Before that? I don’t think so… not in any way that stands out to me. What’s bothering me is really that I did blow up at that younger volunteer over the crooked tablecloth. It was such a small thing, and I don’t usually speak to people like that. I suppose that was the moment where I wasn’t controlling myself the way I should have.\n\nDoctor: Have you been dealing with any unusual stress or life changes recently that might have contributed to how you reacted in that moment?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose there has been something. I didn’t really want to make too much of it, but about a week ago I was robbed outside the bank. Since then, things have felt… off, I guess. I’m not saying that excuses me snapping at the volunteer, but it might have had something to do with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a bit more about the robbery? How long ago did it happen, and how has it affected you since then?\n\nPatient: It was about a week ago now… outside the bank. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was, but since then I haven’t quite felt like myself. That’s really when this all started, I think — this past week, ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you noticed yourself becoming more irritable, anxious, or emotionally reactive than usual?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. I don’t want to make myself sound like an angry person, because I’m really not. But what brought me in was that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I just… I don’t usually talk to people that way. It was embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you found yourself feeling more anxious, down, or emotionally sensitive in general, even outside of the moment with the volunteer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “down,” exactly… I mean, everyone has off days, don’t they? I’ve maybe been a little more on edge than I’d like, but I don’t want to overstate it. Mostly I’ve just felt not quite myself, if that makes sense. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

The thing I keep thinking about is that I did snap at that younger volunteer over the tablecloth being crooked. It was such a small thing, and I raised my voice before I really caught myself. I don’t usually talk to people like that, so… that’s what bothered me enough to come in. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Since the tablecloth incident, have you found yourself feeling more irritable or easily annoyed by small things, even if you didn’t act on those feelings?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s just that one incident that’s been weighing on me. I suppose that means I was more easily annoyed than I’d like to admit, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any other times in the past, even before the tablecloth incident, where you felt you reacted with sudden anger or frustration that seemed out of proportion to the situation, even if it didn’t come up in our conversation so far?\n\nPatient: Not really, doctor… not that I can think of. I mean, what brought me in was that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I just don’t usually talk to people that way. It felt out of proportion after the fact, and I was embarrassed by it. I didn’t really want to make too much of it, but about a week ago I was robbed outside the bank. Since then, things have felt… off, I guess. I’m not saying that excuses me snapping at the volunteer, but it might have had something to do with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a bit more about the robbery? I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was, but since then I haven’t quite felt like myself. That’s really when this all started, I think — this past week, ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you noticed yourself becoming more irritable, anxious, or emotionally reactive than usual?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. I don’t like admitting that, because I’m not usually that sort of man. But what made me come in was I snapped at one of the younger volunteers over a tablecloth being crooked. It was such a small thing, and I spoke sharper than I should have. I felt badly about it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting upset or frustrated more easily by small things in the past week, aside from the incident with the tablecloth?\n\nPatient: Aside from that… I don’t know that I’ve had another big outburst.
